 what up guys? So I have a pretty cool stat to share with you. So I'm pretty proud of this. So I started jujitsu back in 2003 and since that time, I, you know, have won lots of competitions. But you also have longevity in mind and you want to be able to do this stuff for a really long time without getting injured and be able to train consistently. So if that's you, then watch the video and take these seven commandments in and hopefully this is useful to you. Appreciate you guys for being here. Commandment number one, thou shalt remember that none of this fricking matters. So this is one of the most surefire ways to just ruin your experience in jujitsu period is taking this thing to super serious, right? Every training session super serious. Remember, practice doesn't count. This isn't the world championships. This is you rolling with people. This is you playing a game. Even the people that are training at the highest, highest level. Sure, you train super intense, but you don't want to eat submissions. Remember, this is a game that has serious consequences for your body. And being that it's a game, we have a reset button called a tap. You want to be willing to press that reset button often. And if you're one of those people that comes in that takes a thing super serious and you treat every training session as if it really matters, it's going to be hard to press that reset button. And you'll probably find that you fight through submissions way too long, or you go after them too aggressively, not good for training. And so again, I just remind you, none of this matters. Your girlfriend, your boyfriend, your friends, your dad, your mom, nobody cares about your training sessions. They're not that important. And so remember, the training sessions, none of this matters. Commandment number two, thou shalt catch fast and finish slow. So think about it this way. When you have someone in an arm bar as an example, when that arm is fully locked out, and you're applying pressure, that is when they are at the point of maximum danger for that joint. Let's say you've got a hill hook, you've got the hill hook locked in, you've got your legs wrapped around it, you feel the tightness in that knee and that joint. That is the point where we begin to apply pressure with the hips. It's maximum danger. This is where things can go wrong. And so we want to treat that with a certain level of reverence, right? So when you go into those submissions, I, at least this is how I do it, I will be very quick to get the catch. Let's say it's a Kimura. I might get the figure four lock really quick. And then break the grip. But when I'm taking that submission to the end range, where that person is then in danger of having a tendon ripped or a joint popped or whatever it is, I go very slow to the finish. Now I do this for a couple reasons. One is because I want to take care of my training partners. I don't want them to get hurt. I would like them to do the same for me, right? Also too, it is a demonstration of control. For me in the gym, this is my philosophy on applying submissions. I want to apply the submission with as much finesse, technique, leverage, just all those types of things as possible, rather than having to muscle it to grip and rip. And for me, if I can't apply the submission slowly with control and demonstrate that control over my training partner, then my technique needs to get better. Also too, I want my technique to be a little bit better in the gym. So this way, if I go compete, if it gets a little sloppy, it's better than what it needs to be. It reminds me of one of my old boxing coaches. I remember, you know, he would get on us during certain drills to have our hands up a little bit higher than what we needed to. And his whole thing was so that this way they drop where they need to be when your arms get tired. Maybe that's not a great analogy, but that's my thought process. When it gets a little sloppier in competitions or when I was fighting in MMA, I knew that I could rely on my technique because it was much better in the gym. And so I don't apply any sloppy submissions. If I've got a submission and I'm getting ready to flop back, I let go of it if I don't have control. I won't use certain things, certain submissions that I can't apply with control. I just won't touch them because, again, he goes against my philosophy on training to both get really skilled and then also take care of my training partners. But again, that is the idea of the submission. Catch it fast, finish slow. Number three, thou shalt avoid those who eat submissions. So hopefully you don't have anybody in your gym that does this, but I know they exist out there. And I used to run into them when I was younger. We don't have this problem at my gym now. But I remember when I was younger and I would train with some of the MMA bros, you would get these guys that would eat submissions. You put them in an arm lock and they wouldn't tap. I remember one guy literally told me, he's like, I don't tap to submissions. Like I had him in an arm lock and I'm like, Hey, bro, you're going to tap. He said, I don't tap to submissions. So, you know, me being then younger and stuff back then, I just took his back and choked him unconscious, right? But now if I was in that sort of situation and this is what I would recommend, I wouldn't roll with him. I would avoid him. If I had someone that told me I don't tap to submissions or if I had a fully locked in something and they're like grimacing and they're not tapping, I'm just going to stop and go roll with someone else. I'm going to avoid that person because one, I don't want to pop anybody's anything in the gym. And then also two, if this is how you're treating submissions, then I have no confidence that you're going to be safe with me if you get me in something. You want to have this same thing with your rolling where if you're rolling with someone and maybe I'll tell you about how I do it. I roll with someone and I put them into a lock and they may be an inexperienced person moves the wrong way, which could damage them. I let go. If my training partner makes a weird noise or, you know, there's something I just feel something weird about it. I let go or even sometimes I have a really good submission applied and all of a sudden maybe I'm falling off to the side and I realize that I'm either going to have to apply really quick pressure or I'm just going to lose control and possibly injure them. I just let go of it completely. And so you want to have that ability to in the middle of the role, no matter how focused you are on what you're trying to do with the technique, you can stop in an instant. You can stop on a dime. And again, this can take some time and some skill to develop, but you want to develop it. It's something where just like the other day I was rolling with one of our brown belts at the gym and he had me in a butterfly suite and my hand got caught. It got pinned underneath of him and he was taking me over and my wrist was about to break. Right? What did he do when I started yelping? I went, ah, I made like a noise. What did he do? He let go instantly. His old body just went limp and my arm was freed and I was saved from a broken wrist. So he was focused on getting a sweep and he was locked in and ready to go. I was like midair and when I did that, he let go. By the way, Brian, thank you if you're watching this. And so I let go. I don't have a broken wrist and then we got to roll again. So you want to develop this with your training, the ability to just shut off the body in an instant if someone makes a weird noise or you feel something that's out of place. Number five, thou shalt communicate with your training partners. What do I mean by communicate with your training partners? Well, I'll use my arm triangle as a great example. Years ago, my arm triangle was shaky. And a lot of times what would happen is, is I would get it. It wasn't quite where it needed to be. So what I would do is I would just start squeezing harder and harder. And a lot of times I would get a submission from the crank, right? Well, eventually that's not going to work. And so I started to train with people and when they were defending, especially these brown and black belts, I'm trying to go for the submission they're defending, I would stop and ask them, hey, what do you feel? Like, why is this not working? And they could tell me what they felt. And then I would a lot of times make some adjustments and try to troubleshoot it. And then we would figure it out together. And obviously, these are my like, consistent training partners. And over the time, I developed a really strong arm triangle choke, what's one of my best submissions now. And I can get it on people and I mean, it's locked in. It's tight. And this is one of the way that you can tighten up a lot of your submissions. When you're applying them to someone, if they do not tap instead of putting more gas in the pedal squeezing harder, all that stuff. Ask them what's going on. Ask them what they feel that can be incredibly helpful. I had the same thing happen with my leg locks years ago where I'm rolling with a guy put him in a hill. He's got really good leg locks, good leg lock defense. I feel like I'm doing it right. And I even ask him, hey, bro, why aren't you tapping to that hill hook? And he said, oh, because of this, that and that. Ah, I learned something. So instead of just muscling through it, burning my arms out, I'm getting some actual good feedback. And now I can get better. And so one of the things that you can do with your training partners, especially those consistent training partners that you kind of develop a good training bond with. If you're applying a submission, they don't tap instead of trying to rip it more or whatever. Ask them what they feel. And again, if they're grimacing and like fighting away, well, then we avoid that person. But assuming that you're applying the submission and they're just not really responding, then instead of squeezing, burning your arms out, possibly hurting them, you can get more information on how to make that submission tighter, have it more technical, be able to apply with more finesse and less energy and make your jiu-jitsu better. Number six, thou shalt tap fast and tap often. Pretty simple one. We talk about this one all the time in jiu-jitsu, I think, but it's important. I don't care if I'm in some weird position and I shouldn't tap to it or it's not even a real submission. I tap a lot of times in my gym. Again, I'm one of the highest ranking guy. And you know, there's black belts and stuff, but I'm rolling with a lot of blues and purples and stuff. If they get me in a submission, I tap no big deal. And I would tell you that even sometimes if I get put in something like a sloppy neck crank, which isn't like a real submission, and it's something where I could eat it, but I know that especially at my age, I'm 40 now, if I eat that neck crank for too long, like my neck's going to be all jacked up for the rest of the day, or even a couple days later, I just tap to it and just next time do a better job of staying out of those positions. As a quick aside, I personally don't use neck cranks much in the gym for submissions because for me, the juice isn't worth the squeeze, you know, because you're attacking that spine. It's not something that I can apply without putting some pressure on that. I just, I like to stay away from the neck for my training partners. And so I don't use them personally. I just don't think it's worth it. But that said, with any submission that you get caught into or any weird position, whatever it is, just tap. That's what it's there for. Again, it's the reset button. Press that reset button over and over again. If you're playing a video game, just press the reset button, load up your save file, whatever you want to consider it and restart the damn thing instead of eating weird positions or fighting through things you shouldn't when you might feel some torque on your shoulder or whatever might be. So really simple one. Just tap, tap, tap often. Number seven, the last one. Thou shalt honor the golden rule of submissions. So think about this. It's really simple. I think the world would be a lot better if we observed the golden rule of submission. It's really simple. I think the world would be a lot better if we observed the golden rule with just about anything, but you want to take care of your training partners like you want them to take care of you. It's as simple as that, right? When you look at submissions, these are powerful techniques and they should be treated with a certain level of reverence and they should be applied with a certain level of responsibility. === FILE: A Unique Stalling Call to Make BJJ Matches More Exciting [afWsPwiwXbk].txt ===

 Linkistarist��� because CGI 1 was a really exciting event. CGI 2 wasn't as exciting. The female matches seemed to be better with the open scoring and stuff. But this conversation, at least I've seen it a bit, has popped up about how do we make jiu-jitsu exciting. There's something that I think is interesting. When we look at jiu-jitsu matches, and this is something my buddy Brandon McGaffron and the PGF, they do a little differently. And I think it's a good thing. I think it creates more entertainment, and it creates a more exciting match. So when you watch the PGF, they will start to give stalling calls, and they will give stalling calls to people in positions that normally they would not get stalling calls on. So for instance, let's say that you have two people going, and the competitor is on the bottom of mount, and you have a competitor on top of mount, right? One's trying to submit the other. Brandon had structured it in the system to where the bottom person, the person that is defending, they will get stalling calls. So if they're not trying to actively escape, they will then give stalling calls to the person on the bottom. Now, that is a subtle but important shift in thinking and the way that we should be doing this. Because here's a thing to think about. When you get someone that's really good at jiu-jitsu, right? Like when you get someone that's really good, it is tough to submit them if they don't open up. There are tons of classic examples. When you go back and watch some of the old Metamorris, that was an event that happened if you guys are newer to jiu-jitsu, there was this event called Metamorris. That was something that they struggled with because they would do a draw at the end of a 20-minute time limit. It was a submission only for 20 minutes, and then it would just go to a draw. And basically, unless you had two guys that were ready to just go after each other or there was a big skill discrepancy, a lot of times if it just went to the distance, one guy would just ball up instead of getting beaten, right? I mean, that was famously like the Andre Graval match and Huron Gracie match. I mean, Huron just balled up. Super good defensively. It's hard to submit a good black belt when they're not engaging and attacking. A lot of times when you're rolling with someone, the submissions and things like that at the higher levels, they typically happen because they're engaging. They're opening up. There's things that they're doing. Because every time you go for something, there's an exposure. You're making yourself somewhat vulnerable. You can't get something without giving up something. So every time you shoot for a takedown, you go for an escape. You go for a guard pass. You go for a guard sweep. In that moment when you're moving, there's always some vulnerability. And I'm just doing this video because I think it's an interesting way to look at it, right? A lot of times we always penalize the person on top. The person on top is not passing enough. You know, the bottom person can be sitting in guard doing nothing. But we always penalize the person on top. Or the person on top who's, you know, supposed to be passing. And once you're in a situation in a point-based competition where you've scored and that person is down points, there's a certain pressure that's exerted on the person who is down points because of the fact that they're now in a situation where the clock's ticking, they're losing, and they have to be more panicky, not as methodical. They can't set things up like they would want to. And it creates a scramble. Likewise, when someone's passing you, when you feel that feeling of your arms and sort of collapsing as that person's coming down, make sure you start trying to scramble away to a turtle position or something else besides just laying back and letting the person flatten you out. And then finally, when you are passing someone's guard, make sure that as soon as you pass their guard, pin the crap out of them for at least a solid three count to train yourself so that when you go to these point-based competitions, you're not giving up easy points to someone and you're also making sure that you secure your points when it's time for you to score. If you've never had any experience with them, you're like that new wrestler that I'm worried about coming into my gym and blowing his arm out because he doesn't know to tap by the arm bar or zig the, or excuse me, move the wrong way, zig when he should zag or get caught in a shoulder lock and try to fight it and get his shoulder ripped out. It's no different. And I would tell you that as a coach, when we started instituting more leg locks back about 12 years ago, we have a pretty heavy leg lock culture. It's like, we're not like the biggest leg lock gyms, but I think it's really important for people to learn leg locks from the beginning, because I mean, I don't want one of my guys to be a purple belt or brown belt and have this non-existent leg lock game. If they want to compete, or even if they just train somewhere else to where, you know, they're going to get exposed to leg locks. I want them to have that from the beginning. And also for safety purposes, here's what I've noticed. I remember back in the day when we did not train them, we did not drill them. We would every now and then throw them into the mix, but otherwise we sort of just left them alone. When that was going on, I would see more catastrophic injuries because of the exact situations you're talking about. They didn't know they were caught or they tried to escape the wrong way. Since we started training leg locks more, it became more, more a part of our culture. And again, it doesn't mean that every guy in my gym is a leg locker, but they have the basics down. They know when they're caught, they know how to escape some basic stuff. We've had less injuries, way fewer injuries. And also too, I would tell you that it's a lot more fun to play legs when you know, when you're caught, you know, when you're not caught, you know how to escape all that stuff. And so to your point, do you feel the knee bar? If you train knee bars, you will know that you're caught. You will know. It's very clear. It's a clear feeling. You're, your legs locked out. You're unable to escape because your escape options are gone. Just like your arm being locked in position, all of a sudden you try to move. It's all really tight. Boom. And you can feel that before anything happens as far as being injured. Same thing with a heel hook. If it's a well-applied heel hook, you will know when your heel is caught, you'll feel it. You'll feel this joint sort of ratchet up and tighten and you tap out. That's it. And so I would tell you guys, Lauren and anybody else, if your coach doesn't right now go over leg locks and ask them, say, Hey, could we incorporate some leg lock training into the gym? And I would tell you that if for some reason your gym has leg lock training and you're avoiding it, do not avoid it. Don't do it. All right. It's so easy. Again, this whole thing that we do jujitsu wise is to get us out of our comfort zone. And I know you might be worried about leg lock injuries and stuff like that, but look, you got this hand right here, get it up to tap quick, right? Trust me, tap quick. It's not a big deal. And if you have the ability to, to get a little situational training in from the positions would be great. Even if it's an open role, say, Hey, can we start in this leg lock position so I can fiddle around with it? There's a lot of cool things you can do, but don't avoid them because again, like I said, you don't want to be in a situation where one, you eventually maybe compete as a blue belt, purple belt, brown belt, and you, you don't have this, this whole part of your game is non-existent. That's a bad thing. I would tell you that me as a person, like I'm not like the heaviest leg locker, but generally speaking, you know, knock on wood, I've done pretty well against leg lockers and competition because I know how they work. So I documented this in my Master's Vault membership, which is a little thing on my back end of my website. I took home my weight class, the medal of the gold in my weight class. I took home the gold in the Open division. Had some tough guys, had some submissions. Now, you might be asking, like, what exactly is flow rolling, play rolling? How do you do it? Because, again, maybe I do it a little differently than you. So here's the idea. I think of it kind of like almost improv comedy where there's sort of the rule that you have to go with it and you can't say no, right? You have to kind of flow with whatever's happening. So when you're rolling with someone in a play rolling format, at least the way that I do it, the whole idea is that you're trying to keep the movement going. We're not trying to get stuck in a spot. We don't want to offer that next level of resistance. So if I'm gripping someone for a throw or whatever and they break the grip, I have to go with it. I'm not going to, like, and try to prevent that from happening. And there's also a lot of give and take. And so you're constantly maybe going for a sweep and then you let them escape or you pass their guard, but then you let them turtle and then re-guard. Even with submissions, you know, the rule that I do is that if you get a submission, you always leave a little bit of space for the person to escape from there. So you keep the movement going. Now, the rolling that I'm including in this video right now is rolling of me getting ready for the competition. And this is what I'm doing to recover. This is what I'm doing to stay active through the injury. And, you know, it doesn't look like we're moving that hard. But as we all know, jiu-jitsu sometimes is a little deceptive with that. I had a heart rate monitor on at the time and I was keeping my heart rate at, like, in the 130 range, which is a pretty good workout, right? It's not crazy hard, but it's damn good for an injury workout. I'm still setting up my takedowns. I'm still going for my guard passes. I'm still doing my sweeps just like I would. It feels very much the same to me as it does in, say, like, you know, a normal roll. Now, the intensity is not there, but the actual movement feels the same to me. And this is one of the other things, too, because sometimes you're going to have to take some time off completely because, you know, you got injured in a way that requires it. When you come back to training, it's a really good idea to re-acclimate yourself to this stuff. You don't want to go into this stuff going from dead rest for, like, weeks, and then all of a sudden you go straight into training super hard. That's a fast way to get yourself re-injured. I know from my own bearded experience, it's a really good way to get yourself injured again and again. And so instead, you can re-acclimate yourself to the jiu-jitsu movements by doing this type of flow movement. Now, the deal is with this stuff, and here's the biggie. You have to have a good training partner, and you have to be disciplined. You have to find someone that you can roll with who doesn't have to win, and you have to find someone that can roll with you where they don't just end up ratcheting up the intensity. And then you have to be disciplined because you have to remind yourself that you're doing this for a particular reason, and you have to resist the urge to want to go harder because we all do. We all love jiu-jitsu. We all love training hard. That's why we get addicted to this stuff, right? We love rolling hard because it feels great, except, like, the next morning when you're recovering, but, like, during the training session, it feels great. But you have to resist that urge. You have to stay disciplined to do this in a particular fashion, even if you feel better because that's, like, those are, like, the famous last words. Like, I feel pretty good. Let's go a little harder, right? Or one more round or whatever it is because you feel good, and then you get yourself re-injured. You have to dial back and ease up off the gas pedal. I actually have one of my black belts coming back to train soon. He had an injury from weight training, and he's getting back to jiu-jitsu, and I'm going to start him with situational rolling and this play rolling idea for at least four to six weeks before he does any sort of full-on rolling to try to get him re-acclimated so that we can avoid re-injury and get him back up to speed. Now, I said at the beginning of this video that you can make improvement with this. You can actually make progress with this. Some of you guys are like, what the hell? How are you going to do that? One really clear story about this is one of my guys came back from an injury. He was a brown belt at the time, eventually became one of my black belts. And he is like a lapel wizard, gee wizard. That's the kind of guy he is. That's the kind of game he plays. It was on a Saturday class, and I remember rolling with him. And because he's coming back from a surgery and because I'm, like, 50 pounds heavier than him, I wasn't going to put full pressure on him, right? So I told him, I was like, lock me up in this lapel wizardry that you're doing, and I'm just going to fight out of it and, like, not put full pressure on you. I just want to tinker with it. And I figured out this grip of how to shut down his lapel grips that he was playing. And afterwards, I was like, that's pretty neat. So then I went around the rest of the day using it on everybody else, and everybody else was kind of dumbfounded by it. I remember at the end of it, they were like, hey, Chewy, what are you doing? Show me, right? Like, show me. What are you doing? Because that was really irritating. Fast forward later on in the year, I'm competing at the Nashville Open. One double gold there. And I used that grip twice in that competition to shut down people's collar grips and their Bravo grips. And I remember thinking about it. I'm like, damn, I'm glad that I did that flow roll with Nick because here I am using that grip against him. Now, there's another side benefit to this thing, too. Recently on my jiu-jitsu podcast, we were talking with orthopedic surgeon and jiu-jitsu black belt Megan Jimenez. And when we were talking to her, she said this interesting piece that I thought was so important. A physical injury eventually becomes a mental one. Because you think about what it does to us. Anybody that's been injured and been taken off the mats or from physical activity or whatever it is, it puts you in a dark place. It ain't fun. And again, sometimes you're going to have to take rest. But in a lot of cases, the little minor injuries that we have, they don't have to completely sideline us, right? And you think about how much jiu-jitsu begins to mean to a lot of us, right? It becomes partly our friend group. We come in for the people that we get to meet in the community there. We have the training aspect. It becomes a part of our identity. And so if you're able to train through that in some capacity, able to avoid a lot of that, you're able to keep your routine alive, stay in shape, be around the people that you enjoy being around. And when you come back, your body never really stopped doing jiu-jitsu, so you're less likely to get re-injured. I even remember one time doing situational training where my knee was messed up and I couldn't do any sort of driving off my leg. I literally would just defend like this. I would lay on my back and let people try to, like, submit me. And I would just lay on my back and defend using my iron squirrel position in, like, frames. And you know what's interesting? I got so damn good at doing that because I did that for a few weeks while my knee was healing up. And when I came back to regular rolling, I felt, like, invincible from side control. Like, even now, like, when I go to seminars and things like that, I let people pass side control. And I almost never have any trouble with people smashing me or submitting me. And it was because of that situation where I got injured and I didn't want to get off the mats, but I couldn't roll hard because my knee was messed up. So I just laid there and played defensively. And it was a beautiful thing. Now, when I went into that role, I wasn't trying to submit anybody. I wasn't trying to win. My training partners knew that I was hurt. They were still trying to submit me, so it wasn't quite a play role. But, again, it was definitely a, like, sub-maximal effort role. And I got a lot better from it. So, again, what I'm trying to get at here, guys, is that if you get injured, it sucks, it's awful. But there are things that you can do. And if you're getting older, and this is kind of where my mind's at because I'm 41 now, right? So that's kind of where my head is at. I want to train for a long time. And I want to train. I want to be very good. I want to be able to compete. But I also want to be able to pick my kid up. I want to be able to hang out with my family. I want to have longevity in mind, right? And so one of the things that you can do is you can change up the intensity of your rolling where it can literally be play rolling. Or it can be just a type of situational training. Whatever you can do. And that really is the question you have to ask yourself. What can I do? And this isn't just true for old people. It's true for everybody. But young people get away with stuff that maybe us old guys can't. Old women, too. You're included. I know, like, my demographic is, like, 95% dudes. But there's, like, five of you, 5% ladies out there that are rocking with me. I appreciate you. But this is something we have to do as we get older. We have to decide, what can I do? There's something I can do. And then you have to find good training partners that can help you do it. If you have a chance to do this type of rolling while you're injured, great. Or if you have someone that's going through it and you realize they need it, again, there is value in it, both from the person that's training that way through the injury and the person that's rolling with them in a lighter fashion to help them get through it. I speak from experience, right? As someone who's competed and done pretty well for himself and also trained consistently with no injuries from 30 to 41. Knock on wood. So, anyway, Mark and anybody else that's having that kind of question or has that idea, hopefully this has been useful to you. And I appreciate you guys. And talk to you next time. And so I could probably write a book on this, because things change a little bit when you've devoted your entire adult life to doing something, improving it, right? So the things that I thought 15 years ago-ish, there's a lot that's changed. But here's one biggie that I see people suffer from all the time, it's something I did wrong, going into it, caused a lot of problems, and I see it committed over and over again. So when I was younger, I basically equated being like having a good training session by the level of exhaustion I felt at the end of the session. So basically the harder sessions were better sessions. The issue is it's not always true, right? And it reminds me of kind of the way that people chase money, okay? So there's a, stay with me here, I know I'm kind of going all over the place here, but you'll see people, and just in society in general, there is this pursuit of money and material possessions that people go after to make them feel happy, right? But the issue is, and when you really look at it, there is a certain amount of money that kind of takes care of the needs and allows us to do things that make us happy, but when you look at the happiness, the things and all the studies they've done and everything else, these things are like relationships with friends and family, your marriage, your relationship with your kids, obviously. Finding purpose in your life, finding meaning in your work. I mean, these things, having autonomy in your life, a sense of freedom, number of different things, right? These are the things that bring people more happiness, but the issue is those are really hard to quantify, aren't they? Like how do you quantify, do I have a good relationship with my son or my wife? I mean, it's kind of hard to put your finger on. You know it when you feel it, you know it when you see it, but it's kind of hard to kind of put your finger on, but money, cars, boats, things like that, those things are pretty easy to put your finger on. You know when you have them, they're tangible, but a lot of times the things that make you happy about them goes back to the intangibles. Why do you have the big house? Well, because you're gonna have friends over. Why do you, like for me, why do I have land? Because my wife can go play around in the barn and the horses have a garden, my son can run up and down the hills and everything else, and we can sled in the winter. Those are the things that it brings, but you have to think a little bit more deeply about it, and I think people just default to, hey, get money because that's gonna make me happy because I can buy this stuff and they realize it doesn't. Anyway, that said, I think a lot of people in Jiu Jitsu, and this is where I was at, they default to hard sessions mean better sessions. Because again, skill acquisition is a pretty hard to put your finger on that thing because you can train on a given day and do you feel like you've gotten better? Who the hell knows sometimes? You can sometimes feel like you're training really focused, really hard, but you sometimes feel like you're getting worse and sometimes you're feeling getting better. It's really hard to know, but if you wore yourself out and you're laying in a pool of sweat, you damn well know that you had a hard training session, and you can feel like I did something. There's a sense of accomplishment that comes with it. Now, I will say that hard training sessions are necessary at times, and they can be very useful and they can be very productive, but they can also be very just, they can be very useless in a sense of skill acquisition. Great for exercise, maybe not good for actual getting skill. On the flip side, you can have a relatively light training session that's very focused on specific situations and objectives, and you won't be leaving in a pool of sweat, but you could get some really good skill work done. And so that's something that's shifted a lot, and I know that when I was younger, it was always hard, hard, hard, the harder the better. As I got older, the experience that shifted was running into lots of injuries, seeing my students run into lots of injuries because we're going through the ringer all the time, we're training too damn hard, and not getting the results that we'd like. It's like, well, we're training as hard as we possibly can. I don't know that we can push it anymore. We have to do something different. Then it became a lot more of being more focused with training, lots of situational training, being more strategic with training, hard days and light days, skill work only, that kind of thing, right? And so with that, over the course of say 10 years from 30 to 40, as I began to shift more towards that, I can tell you that I won more competitions. I felt like my jiu-jitsu improved more. I feel like I have a better knowledge of jiu-jitsu overall, and I got injured a lot less, right? Like I had multiple surgeries, as like three surgeries as like a 20 to 30 year old, and no surgeries from 30 to 40, knock on wood somewhere. So that would be one of the bigger shifts that I had, which was moving away from hard training all the time, to being focused on more really focused skill work, and being moving in that direction where it could be hard or it could be very light, but it's not necessarily about that, it's about are we actively working towards developing a skill, a position, a technique, something like that. And so to your question, bro, that's one of the things that I held pretty deeply back in the day was hard is better, no pain, no gain, that kind of thing, and light was bad, and then that's shifted around a lot, where it has nothing to do with the heart of the light, that depends on what we're trying to do. It's been that way even with lifting. I remember years ago with lifting weights, I would go into the gym, and it was all about power lifting, strongman, the heaviest steps I could lift. I would tell you that now I lift moderate weight, controlled, a centric, pauses, higher rep ranges, full range of motion. I can lift more regularly using that. I've been able to change my physique in a more effective way, and my body feels better for jiu-jitsu because I'm not putting this heavy load on my joints all the time with heavy ass weight. But I'm still getting a lot of benefit from it with both my strength, because I stay stronger longer because I'm doing these slowest centrics and these pauses and developing isometric strength. And also my body feels better because I'm not banging myself up so much that when I go into the gym, my joints are hurrying or I'm super fatigued, so I'm able to get the benefit of the lifting and then go in and train and have no issues. And so I would tell you that, again, dialing it back and training with a purpose instead of just defaulting to intensity because it feels like I did something, that's one of the biggest shifts. And that can be tough because sometimes the intensity feels good. It feels kind of good to be in a pool of sweat if you're wired like me. And so that was the shift kind of moving away from that. That was big and it was helpful. So anyway, hopefully that helps you out, brother. Anybody else watching this video? I'll talk to you guys next time.

And like, I'm happy to see that even though this person is a more successful competitor, this person is really showing a different level of character to get there. And to me that I like that more and more proud of that. And to me, that's a better thing to see. To me, if you get a person who's successfully competing, but they're showing a lack of potential in the gym, that's more, that's more of a letdown than anything. Right. And so I think that kind of going back around to it, people are impressed by your character that you show going through these different things, not just the outcome. The outcome is a thing. We all like it. We want to see it, but I guarantee you, you lose your match or your, your girl's going to be there to comfort you. Your coach will be there to comfort you. If you win, they're going to be there to celebrate with you. Right. And I can tell you from both my wife and several other girlfriends that I've had who have watched me compete. If I lose, they're there. It's all right, baby. I love you. Or if I win, they're there to celebrate with me. It makes no difference to them as far as our long-term relationship. And so again, it's a misplaced worry. It is young and immature, but it's very normal. You're 20 years old. You're in that stage of life. I imagine like I was where you're insecure. You don't really know who you are yet. You're trying to figure yourself out. You're trying to prove to yourself and to everyone else around you that you're good at something. You're, you're worthy of something. Like you are somebody, right? We go through that all the time as men, right? That's part of our journey, right? When we're in our twenties, we're trying to like figure ourselves out, develop skills, become something. Um, that's a normal thing. And so again, it's, it's a normal thing to have those worries, but I would tell you that as far as the, like the women and even the coaching thing misplaced. So hopefully it helps you brother. Good luck into your competition. And I'll talk to you guys next time.

 What up, guys? So a while ago, so a while ago, so a while ago, I read the book, I read the book, Zen and the art of archery, interesting book with some, some interesting ideas, and I was sharing a couple quotes with my Chukru email subscribers, if you want to become one, you can check the links in the comments down below, but here's two quotes to Chukru that I think are really important, just in general with Jiu Jitsu, but especially if you're new with escapes and defense and all this stuff very important, but this is stuff that I still struggled with to really embody until I was later on in my Jiu Jitsu training. So one of the quotes says, what stands in your way is that you have too much willful will, meaning you're trying to willpower yourself through everything, you're trying really hard all the time, right? At least that's how I interpreted it. The other one is that you, quote, you worry unnecessarily, put the thought of hitting right out of your mind. Now, for me, those are two biggies that just stood out to me. They may not be your problems, by the way. I have my stuff. We all have our stuff. I know what mine is and you know what yours is, hopefully. But for me, I dealt with this a lot where, for instance, when I would have someone in the bottom of side control, I'm trying to push and shove and just like muscle my way through this damn position, right? And one of the biggest breakthroughs, the biggest light bulb moments for me is when I would sort of accept the position when someone would get to side control and they'd get there and I would just sort of, all right, we're here. And I would think about relaxing a little bit and try to find a way to get out of that position like I would anything else, you know, because if I'm in, say, the bottom of full guard and the person's locked, I have my legs locked around them, I'm thinking about grabbing collars and sleeves and I have a method to what I'm doing. And I had been given techniques and ideas and concepts and principles and all this stuff from my coaches. But for some reason, every time someone would get on top of me, I would just start to wig out and push and shove and do crazy stuff. And so I remember when I started learning how to accept the position and relax, my escapes got so much better because I'm more efficient, I'm more relaxed, my breathing's staying steady, and I'm thinking about it like any other position and going back to my actual training versus going on some weird instinct that doesn't help me, right? Now, it goes in tandem with the ability to not worry unnecessarily because many of us, I'm raising my hand here, when we first get into this stuff very early on, for some reason, we do this stupid thing where we come into the gym and we're excited about this thing that we're doing, this new thing that we're having fun with. Ah, this jujitsu thing's going to be so much fun. And then we pervert the whole damn thing by making it so serious, right? Like, you take something that should be fun, you're literally rolling around with a bunch of other humans sweating around and just wrestling, just playing, right? That's what you're doing, it's playing. But for some reason, we want to feel like we have, oh, I got something to show for it. Look, I did good at jujitsu today. I submitted someone today. Probably doing being silly. But I mean, that's the reality. You see people where they get bummed and beat up because they had a bad day of training. I'm like, bro, you had a good day of training. You're training. You don't live in some war-torn country where you're dying of starvation. You're not in a cancer bed in a hospital. You're training jujitsu. It's a good day, right? And you can get better from this with the right perspective. And so, again, for me, a lot of my time when I was in the early phases of my jujitsu, I would worry about losing. I would be stiff and panicky because I didn't want to lose because my little fragile ego just couldn't take it. Or I didn't want to look bad or whatever. And one of the biggest breakthroughs just in my jujitsu in general was being able to not worry unnecessarily. None of this matters, right? Because it's amazing. You know, like when I do a really cool submission and I go home to my wife, she's like, yeah, baby, that's great. I'm really proud of you for your submission. Will you go change the diapers? You know, she doesn't care, really. If I talk to someone who's outside of the gym, they don't care what I'm doing. If I go compete, even then, like, and I'm someone probably for most of you guys, I actually make a living doing this stuff. So, and if I lose a match, guess what? Almost nobody cares, right? That's it. It's not that big of a deal. And when you can start to become mentally and physically relaxed in these positions, your escapes and everything go up tremendously, I would tell you, because now you are relaxed. You're thinking about the technique and you're also thinking about it from a standpoint of not worried about if you lose or win. And that helps to get rid of the panic. And then I would also tell you for every other position, you get a lot more from yourself offensively when you're not worried about the what ifs. Well, what if this doesn't work? Well, who? I don't know. We'll deal with it when we deal with it, right? And if you get caught because you go for something, so what? That's part of it, right? It's part of the game.
